cooperation with taiwan. and certainly we have a strong engagement with taiwan. we have recently modernised this engagement on the economic side. i explained how important is the chips industry of taiwan for us, how important it is for our supply chains, for our security, our technology and other perspectives with export control and investment screening. so we have to continue engaging with taiwan economically and politically. but, to tell the truth, i’m not planning to go to taiwan and as far as i know, there is no travel foreseen at the highest political level of the european union. which does not mean that there is no relationship. recently, some member of this chamber, as far as i know, visited taiwan. and the fact that we maintain our one-china policy, it does not prevent us from intensifying our cooperation with taiwan. and the last message, i want to insist on that. the tensions in the taiwan strait will not go away. this situation will remain challenging and i am sure we will have another debate about this issue. and we should be ready to play smartly in this situation with the strong conviction of our request for peace and stability, on our will to have good relations with taiwan, making it possible with the one-china policy and the preservation of the status quo. i know that this will not make a happy 100% to all of you. and some of you would like me to say to take a tougher stance. we have to be aware that this is a difficult situation in which we have to keep both things at the same time. this is a balanced position. no changes in the status quo by force, continue intensifying cooperation with taiwan and keeping one-china policy. i’m sure this will provide for further discussions.

madam president, the so-called energy market is characterized by the subordination of the interests of the people to those of the big transnational energy companies, with dramatic consequences for their living conditions, including the worsening of inflation, leveraged by speculation on energy. regulated tariffs and maximum prices for energy products, electricity and natural gas, reducing vat on electricity and gas and, at the same time, moving towards taxation of excessive or undue profits of all economic groups with energy production or marketing operations, demanding a structural change in the iberian electricity market, putting an end to the marginalist rule in force, since the so-called iberian exception has not changed this anomaly. these are some of the urgent and necessary measures we are advocating so that the energy situation does not worsen in the coming months. in the name of the market and profits, the eu prefers to keep the stick on households and micro, small and mediumsized enterprises. either they get cold and compromise their business or they empty their wallets. this is the european union's response to rising energy prices.

in writing. the european commission's 8th cohesion report entitled 'cohesion in europe by 2050', presented on 9 february 2022, months after the adoption of the 2021-2027 multiannual financial framework, reflects a number of issues in the union's cohesion policy. it offers a positive economic outlook for the regions, addresses the concerns of europeans about quality of life, employment opportunities and solutions for social inclusion, and ensures a fair sharing of the costs and benefits of responding to new challenges. all this can and should reassure the citizens of the union. as for the challenges for the next 30 years, the report predicts that the growth of the european union (eu) will be driven by the green and digital transitions. these transitions will bring new opportunities but will require significant structural changes that could create new regional disparities. to this end, it is envisaged that between 2021 and 2027, the contribution from the european regional development fund (erdf) and the cohesion fund will be 30% and 37% respectively of the union contribution to the expenditure on climate objectives set in the budget, with the aim of making the eu economy climate neutral by 2050.

madam president, in countries like portugal we have for decades been witnessing the destruction of the productive apparatus and the intensification of depopulation and desertification of vast areas of the country, sacrificing the indispensable foundations for a genuine process of economic and social development. the profoundly asymmetrical impact of the european union's common policies on the member states confirms the need to recover national sovereignty in areas such as industry, agriculture, fisheries and trade, but also energy. the updating of the industrial strategy we have discussed today is nothing new. it lacks, of course, an assessment of the processes of deindustrialization, the causes of which are, in certain countries such as portugal, inseparable from these factors. it does not mention the harmful effects of relocating industrial units to third countries, reducing wages or attacking workers' rights. it maintains the logic of competitiveness and the untouchable single market. it continues to advocate that innovation and r & d activities should be at the service of the major economic groups rather than aligned with the development strategy of each country. while cutting funds in cohesion policy, direct and indirect funding for military industry and research is being increased for the european union, affirming its militarist nature, maintaining its project of training the european army, serving the strategy of nato and the united states of america. we are in favour of a strategy which values national production and the exploitation of the country's potential for the creation of jobs with rights and the enhancement of workers' rights, the fight against external dependence and the affirmation of a sovereign path of development.

the next item is the commission statement on the situation of smes in the union (2022/2823 ((rsp)).
vice-president of the commission. – madam president, this week’s state of the union by president von der leyen took place against the context of the war in ukraine, soaring energy prices, inflation and supply chain bottlenecks. these are prime concerns for our smes and i therefore welcome your initiative to hold a debate on the state of the eu’s smes during the same plenary session. our smes need a lifeline in these unprecedented times. yesterday, president von der leyen announced that the commission will put forward an sme relief package, including a proposal for a single set of tax rules for doing business in europe. we will also combat late payments by revising the late payment directive. let me also say a few words on our ongoing action on three issues that are key for smes: access to finance, regulatory burden and capacity for green and digital transition. given the exceptional challenges, we are ensuring that the funding we will provide can deliver most impact for smes. among such funding, the single market programme will provide eur 1 billion for smes’ support. it pools key activities such as the enterprise europe network, the joint cluster initiative and erasmus for young entrepreneurs. the programme also proved flexible to meet evolving needs, such as the support to ukrainian smes to help them integrate into the single market. we helped, setting up the supply chain resilience platform for companies in the eu or ukraine to retrain, restructure or replace existing supply chains to keep production going, because our single market is a formidable level for expansion and a safety net for eu business, but also for our partners. our flagship investment programme, investeu, will help smes access loans and equity, including tackling solvency risks. investeu will also support smes that are going public or intend to do so. this will attract additional private investments to help smes scale up and grow. now coming to smes business environment. entrepreneurs will tell you and tell us that they need legislation to be clear, easy to implement and avoid disproportionate costs. we have committed ourselves to removing red tape and lowering costs without compromising policy objectives. better regulation remains a key priority for the commission. under the direction of my colleague, vice-president maroš šefčovič, we are assessing impacts on smes and we are reviewing legislation as the fit for future platform and we are filtering initiatives regarding their relevance for smes. and we need your help as the co-legislator to keep burdens as low as possible. finally, the transition to a more sustainable way of doing business, the implementation of the green deal and digitalisation of smes. this requires a joint effort. to advise and support the smes in green transition we introduced the sustainability advisers provided through the new enterprise europe network. the transition pathways we just discussed in the previous debate will for each industrial ecosystem identify specific constraints faced by small businesses via a set of semi-specific questions. on the digital transition, the digital europe programme supports smes with access to technical expertise and testing, including the possibility to ‘test before invest’ via european digital innovation hubs. it also helps smes’ employees to become proficient in areas such as artificial intelligence, cybersecurity or blockchain. together, that is with you and the council, we can make sure that the twin transitions move forward with the minimum burden and costs on smes. it is our collective responsibility. with that, madam president, honourable members, i come to the end of my statement. let us all go back to work for europe’s smes, which diligently deliver so that europe can become more resilient.
on behalf of the ppe group. – madam president, madam commissioner, dear colleagues, small and medium-sized enterprises are the backbone of the european economy. but right now, their situation is alarming. the fallout from the covid pandemic and the war in ukraine, together with increasing administrative burdens, means that the situation for smes is only getting worse. simply put, our smes are not getting the support they need. this is why we need a dedicated sme envoy in the commission to defend smes and make sure that all new legislation is sme—friendly. we also need to implement the one-in, one-out principle like the commission has promised many times, so that smes are not overburdened by new rules and regulations that would hinder their growth and innovation. it’s regrettable that the commission has dragged its feet for so long in this matter. while i also welcome president von der leyen’s proposed sme relief package, it is simply not enough. it is the time for the commission to bring forward a comprehensive sme package that addresses these issues. strong smes are key for new innovations, economic growth and the green transition in europe. now the commission must get to work and act for our european smes.
i think that in this moment of great economic crisis and also of the production system, we really must put, beyond all the rhetoric, all the phrases we keep repeating, as a priority the help, the support, the attention, the calibration of our choices for smes. the big champions, which we also need in europe, are not enough. it is this widespread framework that we must involve and support and we must do so also with the help of the large companies. the virtuous companies involve all these people in their chain, made up of suppliers, contractors and so on, in their positive processes of innovation, of finding new solutions, in short, of quality, and they manage to drag the whole chain along in this leap in quality that they make. here, let's make this process work. and then for small and mediumsized enterprises we know very well what the priorities are: the president made some remarks yesterday: we must be more timely, more relevant, not just words, but some real concrete action on the subject of energy for small and mediumsized enterprises, the subject of training of staff, the lack of it, the delay in payments. i think that there is something drastic and strong in this too, which helps the member states not to be in default because the member states are in default. the directive has not been implemented, so i think it is really time to be proactive: the commission has already made a number of proposals.

on behalf of the ecr group. - madam president! - i have to go. we all agree that smes are the backbone of the european economy in almost all sectors, including in the area of digitalisation, which is often forgotten when we think of these large companies. it is also a huge number of family businesses that deserve our special attention. unfortunately, despite the declarations that we often hear in this room, the situation of small and medium-sized enterprises after the pandemic and in the face of rising energy prices continues to deteriorate. in addition, their activity in the single market is limited by unnecessary non-tariff barriers and protectionism, especially as regards transport companies and small service enterprises. many entrepreneurs are struggling with the increasing bureaucracy and the overly complex mechanism of raising european funds, which prevents them from adapting quickly to the requirements of the digital economy. when designing new legislation, we need to pay more attention to these issues. small and medium-sized enterprises will create innovative products and services if we create the right conditions for them. when we work on the ai act or the data act, we should keep this in mind and keep the administrative requirements for small and medium-sized businesses and the costs associated with them to a minimum.

on behalf of the s&d group. madam president, just over two and a half years ago, the european union was struck by the greatest pandemic in a hundred years, which affected everyone in the world, without exception. the eu responded with the tools of the day, but we soon realised the need to build a true european health union. this regulation is thus the culmination of several months of intense negotiations, with the principles of 'one health' and 'health in all policies' at its core, recognising that health is an integral part of any prosperous society. this regulation provides for: the creation of prevention, preparedness and response plans at national and european level; the recognition of public health emergencies; greater coordination and coordination with international bodies such as the world health organisation, as crises know no borders; stress testing and training at various levels for health professionals; more transparency; reference laboratories and reduction of the administrative burden for those managing the crisis; multi-sectoral collaborations and a clear focus on health literacy and the fight against disinformation. this is a historic moment. we have a more robust mandate, because in a crisis, we are only as strong as our weakest link. a final word of thanks to those who made all this possible: to the commission, to the two presidencies who worked with us and, in particular, to you, veronique, and to the whole team of shadow rapporteurs, for having been able to rise to the challenge. for a european health union that leaves no one behind.
madam president, when we discuss ocean governance it is essential that we mention the outermost regions. these are the factors which allow the european union as a whole to have the largest maritime area in the world. therefore, their enormous potential is and should be fully exploited, whether in terms of producing renewable energy, harnessing the potential of biodiversity in these regions or implementing innovative and sustainable pilot projects that change the way society deals with the environment. it is not just a question of asking for exceptions to take account of their specificities, but also of empowering and providing the necessary regulatory and financial basis for the outermost regions to take the lead in the climate transition within the european union itself.
the rapporteur. - madam president, the european economy is changing. if it is true that the difficulties are more present, it is no less true that opportunities are also arising. europeans, especially young people, are now looking for alternatives to saving and investing. with the emergence of new technologies, financial markets have also found new solutions, solutions that are becoming more and more decentralized, more accessible and more present in our lives. policy-makers are not just asked to respond to immediate crises. in the complex, volatile and fast-paced environment in which we live, there is a greater need than ever for smart policymaking and forward-looking solutions. we have a duty to make european law more innovation-friendly so that national law is also a dynamo of progress. this conviction is particularly important when it comes to taxation. taxation is a key instrument for a policy to promote innovation, competitiveness and growth. unfortunately, they are often an obstacle to the efforts to fill the coffers of the state at the expense of the sacrifices made by families and businesses. as a portuguese woman, i am well aware of this phenomenon, the stifling tax burden that is tearing apart our financial freedom and hijacking our future. a pro-innovation, competitive and future-oriented tax policy cannot ignore the emergence of new technologies such as blockchain or the multiplication of crypto-assets. to enhance europe's opportunities in this area, we have put forward these proposals on the impact of new technologies on taxation. what are these proposals? firstly, the clear statement that tax policy is the responsibility of the member states and that we should not tax assets at any cost to fatten national budgets. it is up to each of the 27 states to decide on the taxes they collect. but in an internal market with fair and healthy competition, there must be a minimum of cooperation. when it is decided to tax an asset, the tax must be fair, clear, transparent, predictable and proportionate. secondly, national tax authorities also have to adapt to the challenges of digitalisation. we want a clear increase in human and material resources, a programme of training for staff at european level and the creation of a platform for the sharing of good practice between countries. thirdly, we call for coherence between european law and national law and, for the time being, between national laws. the definitions of 'crypto-asset', for example, or 'taxable event' or 'permanent establishment', must be common, otherwise we will treat similar situations differently. fourth, we want blockchain technology to be harnessed to the full extent of the opportunities it represents. the characteristics of this technology allow us to combat tax fraud and evasion more quickly and effectively and can also make procedures more efficient, reducing red tape for citizens and the costs of context for businesses. finally, we want the union to lead the dialogue towards an international instrument on the taxation of crypto-assets. it is pointless to legislate from one country to another, a reality which knows no borders. if competition is healthy, it must also be fair and just. the change we are experiencing in the economy and in the financial markets cannot be stopped. we will face complex challenges, but we will also meet challenging opportunities. it is up to us to live up to the time when we are called upon to decide, and we will do so with our eyes on the future.
the rapporteur. - madam president, the political debate is a field of disagreement. but it is important to stress that when we talk about new technologies and taxation, there is a broad consensus with clear statements, as we can hear in the speeches throughout the debate. the first consensus is that taxation has to be smart, has to be innovation-friendly and future-oriented. the second consensus is that new forms of investment and saving are extraordinary opportunities for everyone, but they should not be windows of opportunity for tax fraud and evasion. the third consensus is that our fiscal policies and our tax authorities need to adapt quickly to the challenges of the digitisation of the economy and the decentralisation of financial markets. finally, there is a consensus that taxes are national, that european cooperation is essential and that an international agreement is essential. the times we live in, as i also had the opportunity to say earlier, are difficult and are complex. we're all seeing our bills go up. families see their mortgage payments rise month after month, and businesses are naturally insecure with rising interest rates and the breakdown of supply chains. so now is the time to use tax policy to help families and businesses overcome the crisis we are already facing and to anticipate the challenges of a future in which new technologies will be a daily reality. we must therefore be up to date with our times, but above all with the future we want and which europe must lead.
in writing. the report aims to allow greater flexibility to deal with the consequences of military aggression by the russian federation (flexible assistance to territories) fast-care. this proposal was announced at the july plenary session and was referred to the committee on regional development by commissioner elisa ferreira. the proposal considers the impact of russian military aggression and provides responses through a series of flexibilities, which concern several key aspects that also affect the outermost regions, such as madeira and the azores, such as the cohesion policy programmes for 2021-2027 that should strengthen the competitiveness of local economies through the development of tourism and agricultural production or responses to the basic needs of citizens through financing, housing, water supply, waste management, renewable energy and health infrastructure, as well as training and education. the whole process was a simplified procedure, from the presentation of the commission proposal to the vote on the european parliament's plenary session, which lasted about three months. the rapporteur stressed the urgency of the entry into force of this regulation and recommended that parliament should approve it without amendments.
madam president, for more than ten years we have heard arguments about the dangers that a common charger would pose to innovation. this is a common prejudice, but rarely more than that. the innovation we are interested in is one that improves the quality of life of people and respects the limits of our planet. different chargers just brought us tons of e-waste, preventing us from using our old chargers or borrowing from a friend and filling our drawers with useless cables, as alex showed us here today. it has been more than demonstrated that industry would never create a common charger on its own and that innovation must be guided, as we have done here today, in the direction that benefits people and the environment. from 2024, we'll have the innovation we need, upgrading the charger whenever there are faster or more energy-efficient solutions. parliament can now be proud that we have got rid of the jungle of cables in which we used to live.
on behalf of the ppe group. madam president, the variety of living beings on earth is one of its greatest treasures, and biodiversity is a fundamental asset that we all have a duty to preserve. the question for governments and the commission is whether we are doing all that is necessary to safeguard the future of the planet and whether we can not only protect more species, but whether those already identified as being at risk and in need of protection are actually protected. we cannot tolerate the constant attack on the planet's biodiversity by those who want to keep endangered species as pets just for fun. we must ask whether, in particular, animals and plants which are victims of trafficking or illegal trade are being adequately dealt with by governments and their border controls, and whether the criminals are being punished appropriately. all elements in the trafficking chain must be subject to a firm and unrelenting response. more than amendments to conventions, which are also necessary, we need to strengthen the capacity of the judiciary to monitor and respond.
mr president, commissioner, in this question entitled 'combating depopulation through an instrument of cohesion policy', we all know that depopulation, ageing and rural abandonment are certainly among the main policy objectives of the commission and the member states. it is certainly a european priority, but how do we do it? we need to involve the people of the regions, the living forces, the municipalities, we need to create jobs - we all know this - public services, access to very high-speed internet networks, innovate in mobility, preserve the landscape, biodiversity, promote economic activities. the transition to the future has to be digital -- we all know that -- has to be green, but it also has to be demographic. and in fact - and this is the question - why should we, commissioner, not pre-assess the demographic impact of all european policies, especially in the area of cohesion and rural development? because if we don't, we're always putting money in, but the results aren't what we expect.
commissioner, we have discussed this a lot, and i cannot agree more with the commissioner and the sensitivity we acknowledge. but do you see that in europe and perhaps in each of the member states - and we are talking about our own member state, which we know well - is it true, as the commissioner has said, that the important centres close to the large capitals do not develop because the large capital attracts, and the other large spots of the territory, in the specific case of the low-density regions? that is why there is a lack of poles of attraction, and that is why i think cohesion policies should also be very targeted and very clear for those poles, precisely because this helps to balance each of the member states.
madam president, mr president, minister, commissioner, it should not be necessary to remind you that european democracies are based on fundamental values, including respect for the rule of law. but, yes, we have to! there are countries that are outside the rule. they actually try to convince us that they have a different view. but we all know that the rule of law is: separation of powers, independence of the judiciary, fight against corruption and respect for fundamental rights, for minorities, for freedom of expression and the press. we have a very strong foundation, which leaves us no doubt: the treaties of the european union. this regulation must be complied with in its entirety. the commission must be demanding and do everything possible to ensure that the european institutions, the european parliament and the citizens can have confidence in it. for example, it is not enough to say that hungary will set up an independent authority. it must ensure that this authority is in fact independent in its constitution and functioning and independent of the judicial system. this is the key point.
madam president, mr minister, mr commissioner, we are talking here about a long history of denunciations of corruption, of attacks on the rule of law, of attacks on freedom of the press and freedom of expression. and we are talking about a long way to go in building a mechanism capable of protecting the european budget from this kind of attack. we have reached a point where we cannot accept half measures, nor can we accept half measures. it is our credibility, the credibility of all of us, of the european union, of the european commission and of the european council, that is at stake. our voters, our european citizens, our taxpayers will not forgive us if we take measures which are only half-measures and which are mere promises and mere make-up operations in order not to cut european funds and not to apply this mechanism. gentlemen, we are running out of time. it's now or never!
mr president, the death of mahsa amini at the hands of the iranian morality police, simply because she had the islamic veil wrongly placed, is a case of intolerable and shocking violence and restriction of the most basic rights of women. it's one of those cases that we thought no longer existed in our world, like female genital mutilation. but here they remain between us. more than our solidarity, today is our cry that joins all the iranian men and women who have since then, with great courage, demonstrated for their rights and for an end to repression in their country. mr high representative, the european union must continue to demand respect for women's rights in international institutions and in all its interactions with third countries, in development aid or in diplomatic missions. as this parliament has been advocating, our foreign policy must be a real weapon to promote and defend them throughout the world.
mr president, about ten years ago i was in iran. our guide, a young woman, shared with us the terror in which the young women lived. a lock of her hair would have been enough to escape from her veils. they lived in daily confrontation, on the way out of the house, on the way out of the university, with the so-called spontaneous morality groups. we have not forgotten the cost of the 1979 revolution for iranian women, when they had previously fought for freedom. today we are witnessing a morality police force which wants to force women to hide their bodies, which takes away their rights and freedoms, which kills them. the european union's concrete solidarity with these women, as well as with those men, women and young people who are finally, with great courage, standing up for fundamental values, is fundamental and urgent. not just the values that are rooted in european culture. we are talking about values and universal rights.
madam president, commissioner, i listened to your speech very carefully and it is clear that the promotion of stability in the horn of africa is essential to promote stability in the whole region and security and stability in europe as well. but i have not heard you speak about a very important issue at the moment, which is precisely the freedom of navigation in the red sea and the gulf of aden, which is crucial for energy flows, for european energy security and for the stability of security in the region. we all know the kind of criminal practices that exist in this area and so i would like to hear from you about the very worrying non-renewal of the un resolution on piracy and about operation atlanta, which was of crucial importance in promoting security in this area. i would also like to hear from you on the european strategy to deal with the growing dependence of african states on chinese investment and also on the russian presence, the presence of russian mercenaries who are increasingly creating instability in the region. this is a strategy for which we must count on the cooperation of the african union and the african states.

mr president, the scale of the tragedy in pakistan is undeniable and calls for our full solidarity and for the european union to step up humanitarian aid. we know very well that what has happened is the result of the climate change we are experiencing and the importance of combating this climate change. but in an emergency, you have to do more. in the face of the numerous examples of extreme situations that climate change is placing societies in, with increasingly catastrophic results, as is the case in pakistan, the european union needs to lead the global movement to adapt our urban structures and prevention plans to these types of threats. the european union must encourage this project, which must involve all the experts, in order to try to be one step ahead of the problems, rather than always reacting to the tragedy.
mr president, in recent times agricultural producers in portugal have been exposed and vulnerable to many natural phenomena and disasters, but they have long been confronted with the impositions of a cap which disregards small and medium-sized farming, which promotes and finances agribusiness, intensive and superintensive agriculture, which consumes more water, fertilisers and plant protection products, which destroys more soil, without an economic and social development which favours the territory and the people, rather than serving the interests and profits of the economic groups operating there. the consequences of the war, its instigation, the sanctions and the speculative actions and exploitation by the big economic groups have recently dragged small and medium-sized agricultural production into the street of bitterness and nobody wants to stand up for them. it is immoral that the same people who, during the pandemic, saw their profits grow at the expense of the suffering of thousands of people, now use the pretext of war to satisfy their insatiable hunger. we know that since last year, fertilisers have gone up by as much as 300%. the war has exacerbated the tendency to speculate on price increases, with the consequent rise in production costs. it is urgent to question the production model and to change the logic of production based on profit to production based on need, to guarantee the right to national production, particularly in the field of fertiliser production, to guarantee the right to food sovereignty and to apply the principle of national preference by replacing imports with national production, to guarantee fair prices for production to small and medium-sized farmers, to shorten trade circuits, and to intervene and regulate the value chain.

on behalf of the s&d group. madam president, commissioner, mr nicolas schmit, ladies and gentlemen, at the porto social summit, following a good proposal from the european commission put forward by commissioner nicolas schmit, european leaders committed themselves to reducing the number of people at risk of poverty by 15 million, including 5 million children. portugal has not only held the porto summit, where these objectives were enshrined, but has also already set up the child guarantee or the free daycare network. we would like to see the plans of many more countries come off the page, because with the energy crisis caused by putin's war, we risk seeing poverty increase instead of decrease. are we waiting for new initiatives from countries like portugal or the spanish presidency of the council of the european union to move forward with a new social summit that will put the promised measures into practice? we don't have that luxury. winter is upon us. on this international day for the eradication of poverty, in the face of the social emergency, we remind the commission and the council of their responsibility. we need to act now.
madam president, commissioner, on this day for the eradication of poverty, i would first like to recall a very sad reality: in europe, just over 10% of citizens live below the poverty line. the european policies we have put in place in recent years, notably with initiatives such as the mobilisation of €80 billion under the european social fund in 2014, have nonetheless been successful in reducing this trend. this is proof that our european model of a social market economy has a number of weapons to fight poverty. i consider that work, a paid job, is probably the best safety net. but we can see today, with covid and the war in ukraine, that this safety net is no longer enough because even those who work face serious difficulties, especially because of the cost of fuel. so it is our responsibility, more than ever, at european level, to find solutions, particularly to combat the price of energy.
madam president, i would like to thank you. poverty is not a natural law. inequality, social and economic disparities are man-made. it is gradually tearing europe and our societies apart. the gaps are widening and the rich are getting richer. today i would like to pay particular attention to women and children and to those workers who can barely afford to put food on the table - those who are in fact day labourers. many women in europe are unemployed or part-time workers and are also usually paid less than men. at the same time, millions of children in europe live below the poverty line and in exclusion. that is why we must adopt a policy which equalises the conditions in which children are brought up. this requires political will and a gradual redistribution of responsibilities within the member states. we will do this by creating more jobs, providing good education, secure employment and trade unionism, and a better safety net for those who need it most. combating poverty is our common responsibility, from the european level to the local level. europe can do better than that.
madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, if you had to choose between turning on the heating or eating, what would you do? this is the question that millions of citizens throughout the union are asking themselves. already today millions of families live in poverty. rampant inflation and the energy crisis risk raising that number dramatically. the whole of europe, from this house to governments, has an obligation to act to prevent a genuine humanitarian emergency and it must do so now. the council discusses proposals to combat the energy crisis, discusses price caps, levies on extra profits, market reform. all of these measures are right, but they do not meet the urgent needs of the most vulnerable citizens. the pandemic has taught us this: the way out of the crisis is solidarity. the divisions in brussels must not be paid for by the people in distress. we need a common european fund which gives priority to support for households and micro and small enterprises. and, i would add, the stability and growth pact: your suspension must be extended. states need the fiscal space to finance large support packages. we don't have time to waste. winter is coming.

madam president, dear colleagues, dear commissioner, in 2021, like said, 95 million people in the eu were at risk of poverty and social exclusion. this was equivalent to 21% of the eu population, and the risks are growing with the current energy crisis and inflation rate. we are the developed part of the world, but many of our citizens have difficulties in paying their bills at the end of the month. the risk of poverty increases, according to eurostat, if the individual is a woman, has children, is young or unemployed. for instance, 27% of young adults aged between 18 and 24 are affected by poverty, and the risk varies across the eu. romania, bulgaria and greece reported the highest shares of people at risk of poverty and social exclusion. dear colleagues, our speeches in the plenary would not resolve the problems. we need, therefore, a european strategy against poverty, with concrete milestones, financial mechanisms and proactive policies implemented at all levels. ‘no one left behind’ should mean something, and this implies effective measures and clear targets to reduce poverty in all our regions. so, i support what commissioner schmit said, that we need to act together now in order to end poverty in europe.
madam president, the international day for the eradication of poverty, which is being marked today, brings to light worrying data on poverty in portugal. according to eurostat, at the end of 2021, 2.3 million were poor or at risk of poverty or social exclusion, which is equivalent to 22.4% of the portuguese population. between 2020 and 2021, poverty has worsened, not only in portugal but in the european union of 27, and the figures for 2022 will be even more worrying. as the rich get richer, the unfair distribution of wealth and inequality increases. the increase in wages and pensions, the fight against precariousness and the defence of employment with rights, the strengthening of public services and their universal access, public housing policies are fundamental, and these are measures which, combined with the taxation of large capital and the progressivity of tax systems, could reverse the trend of poverty that we are experiencing today. but they are not coming soon, because they are contrary to the neoliberal logic which has dominated the policies of the member states of the european union for decades.
on behalf of the left group. mr president, the report to be voted on on the guidelines for employment policies contains some important agreements which improve the commission's proposal in terms of working conditions, wages, public services and the fight against poverty. it also has other interesting formulations on the role of parliament in this process and, in particular, on the european parliament's equating it with the council in all matters relating to the european semester. but it is in the disagreements around this report that we find the most enlightening debates, and it is indeed enlightening to see how the european right and far right are opposed to parliament having a role equivalent to that of the european council and how they advocate, for example, that companies which benefit from public support under the prr or the community budget, can take that public money and transfer it directly to the shareholders, without even having to ensure the maintenance of employment and working conditions for their workers. so the european right wants public money to go to finance companies that lay off workers and take that money and transfer it directly to the shareholders without going through the starting house. the right has also been the most vocal in its opposition to highly equivocal formulations on retirement pensions, such as those which focus on active ageing and which very poorly disguise plans to increase the retirement age, i.e. to impose on workers the costs of the crisis which have not reached everyone, especially those responsible for them.
mr president, commissioner schmit, 22% unemployment by 2030: that is the objective proposed by the european commission in these guidelines. it's a shame, you know? one in four unemployed workers. is this all that the european union can offer us? one out of every four of us on the street? but at the same time, the 2030 agenda talks about zero poverty. zero poverty? what is that? how did you do that? the intentions are clear: subsidy culture. you create poverty and then you market aid. let's change course, employment is the best social policy. let's talk about creating wealth, competitiveness, a market of prosperity where we can earn with dignity, with our effort, the sustenance of our families and be free (and not, as proposed here, dependent on any government).
mr president, the report is a road map for new harsh anti-worker measures under the pretext of the pandemic and the imperialist war in ukraine. in the name of the highly competitive labour market, it seeks to shield european monopolies in international competition in the face of the emerging capitalist crisis. behind declarations without any objection and guaranteeing decent wages and collective bargaining are hidden the frightening anti-working class reforms at the behest of the european union, such as the hatzidakis law in greece, the dismantling of the daily working time is promoted, the definition of wages as a minimum by the criteria of the household basket of products for the poor, the generalization of telework and contract slavery, the mobility of workers, as well as the free shot to social security. in this context, the persecution of workers fighting for their rights is escalating, such as the strikes of grassroots trade unions in italy, the 'demonstration trials' in greece, the conscription of workers in refineries in france. the anti-people policy of the european union-governments-monopolies is a cause of war for the workers and it is necessary to escalate the struggle with the all-union strike in greece on 9 november.
mr president, commissioner, our debate comes at a difficult time in france, where a social conflict is threatening our entire economy, leading to a fuel shortage. this is a real blow for workers and businesses, already suffering from the energy crisis and inflation. in the current situation, in france, but also elsewhere in europe, supporting employment is essential and is our first line of action to fight poverty. in europe, unemployment figures have fallen slightly in the past year. however, young people are still the most affected and have difficulty entering the labour market. it is therefore more imperative than ever to focus on training to develop the skills of workers of today and tomorrow. the matching of training with the needs of enterprises is really essential for the proper functioning of the labour market: it is the best way to combat unemployment. as the spectre of recession looms over europe, we must redouble our efforts.
mr president, dear colleagues, the commission is putting forward the document like only the existing challenges are to be solved: gender pay gap, tackling undeclared work, youth employment, etc. but being previously the rapporteur on guidelines, i agree that these are certainly important issues, but these are not the main challenges the employment market will face in the upcoming period. in—work poverty: this will become a real disaster and not only for poorer eu member states like mine, latvia, but also for western and central european countries. the problem of uncontrolled employment of third—country nationals will become screaming very soon. and finally, of course, drastic job loss. we have to look for new solutions. we may come up with an ambitious proposal for a minimum income directive, not only the minimum wage directive. we also have to start discussions about unconditional basic income. one thing is absolutely clear: in an unusual situation, the usual solutions will not work.
member of the commission. – mr president, i agree with you that in the current cost of living crisis, we need to support workers; we have to help companies and ensure decent working conditions for all. strengthening the social shield is more necessary than ever. the updated employment guidelines are, in this respect, a very important element and can serve as guidance for member states to overcome the crisis we are facing and especially to improve social cohesion. this afternoon, we had a very important debate on poverty eradication. well, to eradicate poverty, the first and best way to do so is good—quality jobs with decent wages. yes, we have to invest in our economy, but we also have to invest in people – investing in people, in their skills, but also investing in gender equality, in diversity, which is a decisive element of inclusive labour markets. all that starts with children – because if children are lost, if children do not get the right opportunities, well, what happens then? once they are older and they try to get into the labour market, they will not be able to find good jobs. they will not have decent wages. and we are going in the direction of more exclusion, of more inequality. this is an important part of good labour market policies: start with children. without that, there are no inclusive labour markets and europe will not be able in the coming years and decades to build a green and digital economy. yes, digital skills are key. and where and when should you learn digital skills? yes, certainly, lifelong learning, but the best is to learn when you are young, when you are at school, and to give older children the right opportunities. labour markets are changing and there is a broad understanding that equality and fairness, but also good working conditions, are essential for a strong, innovative european economy that benefits all.

in writing. it is impossible to have this debate without bearing in mind that the guidelines for the employment policies of the member states are part of the european semester process, which usurps sovereign powers of the states, which imposes, conditions and controls democratic and sovereign political options in the field of budgetary policy, but also social and labour policy, a process that functions as a check on the implementation of the stability and growth pact and the budget treaty. this intimate relationship and the threats, including sanctions, that flow from it are responsible for years of economic stagnation, deregulation and regression of labour and social rights, the deepening of inequalities, the limitation of public investment or the liberalisation of strategic sectors. we do not exclude the responsibility that the european union and its policies have had for the deterioration of working and social conditions, the increase in exploitation, unemployment and poverty, the reduction of wages, which is the path that the right wing of this parliament wants to follow in this report.
mr president, in portugal this weekend tens of thousands of workers, young people and pensioners took to the streets in lisbon and oporto. they do not accept the degradation of wages, the degradation of pensions, the attack on public services, the speculation in the prices of essential goods and energy. they shouted loudly that the cost of living is rising and the people can't take it. at the same time, billions of euros are being accumulated in the profits of economic groups, food, energy, large retailers and multinationals. the increase in social injustice is unacceptable under any circumstances, and even less so in this particular situation. we welcome the workers and their unions for this further initiative of strength, which is at the same time a sign of hope, of confidence, of the possibility of another way. and we reaffirm our commitment to workers and their struggle to defend and improve their working and living conditions.
mr president, commissioners, council and members, stopping populism in europe is helping families in their time of suffering. the european parliament has already said that it is not possible to make a decision on the future of the community without the involvement of the european parliament. we need a european response so that no one is left behind, otherwise we will all end up suffering, because we are just a market and only the populists will laugh again. let's learn from what we did wrong in the financial crisis and what we did right in the covid crisis. we saved europe with more europe in the covid crisis. we will use what we already have and we will build a european response to this crisis and to the future. and let us do so in a cohesive and fair way by building a single energy market, buying and transporting energy together across europe, stopping speculation in energy markets together and looking for resources where they are. it's accumulating in the hands of the super rich, it's accumulating in the super profits of many corporations. and please, don't play games with those who are suffering the most. who would think of asking the ecb to raise interest rates even more? does the epp not know the reality of the lives of europeans, their suffering? don't you know how much it costs to heat houses and buy food these days? did i really have to ask the ecb to inflict more pain on households, raise interest rates more, leading to higher housing loans? is the epp for families and jobs or are we back to the time of the epp moralists who invented austerity as a response to the financial crisis? what's up, p.e.e.?
mr president, one of the main economic and social consequences of the war was the return of the logic of austerity. and unfortunately, it is not only in the epp that we see this return. in portugal, wages and pensions will have one of the largest reductions in real terms in recent years and the largest decline in the weight of wages in national income in the history of our democracy. and this is what is happening to the socialist government, the same government which opposed, and will only be dragged along, until the last minute, the taxation of the extraordinary profits of the energy companies and which has just now decided to reduce the taxation of the large companies. the same socialist government whose finance minister has just supported the raising of interest rates, the epp is not alone in this, and so what we are seeing is the return, in some parts of europe, of the recipes which failed spectacularly in the response to the financial crisis and which are being adopted again by governments of the most varied political families.
mr president, historical levels of inflation go beyond numbers or statistics. they show us the difficulties of families without money to meet their basic needs. the increase in the price of food, energy or transport is throwing families into despair, families who have a large part of the month left over at the end of their salary. and the critical phase of winter is yet to come. but there are already many people who cannot afford to heat their homes, living in situations of real energy poverty. and that is why, more than regrets, we need concrete measures. limits on prices or taxes on extraordinary profits must be concerted measures at european level and must be designed to protect households and businesses, to ensure the well-being of people and to keep our economy running. a final word to the national governments, and in particular to the portuguese: they cannot limit themselves to superficial measures and blame the international situation. much more is required, political responsibility is required in the management of this crisis.
mr president, commissioner, colleagues, mental illness affects all social strata and all age groups and has only worsened with the covid-19 pandemic. it's now the second leading cause of morbidity, and over the last two years, symptoms of anxiety and depression have more than doubled. the question that is being asked here is, fundamentally, whether we are going to turn on the light and see the problem that we have in front of us or whether we are going to remain in the dark without giving an answer to the citizens. i call here for a real debate on this public health issue and for us to bring in more legislative initiatives to combat stigma, improve access to mental health care and contribute to the reintegration of all citizens into society in a healthy and functional way. ladies and gentlemen, let us not ignore the citizens, from the youngest to the oldest. we must face the problem head on, we must do more and we must work here for a real european strategy on mental health. i say here, we turn on the light.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on industry, research and energy. – madam president, let’s be realistic. i mean, what the council has been suggesting is that no less than 40% of all the suggested cuts fall on horizon europe. so 40% is on innovation – the innovation we need for everything we are going to endeavour in the coming years. if you look a little bit closer, it is just 7% of the budget, but you impose 40% of the cuts. i mean, if you give a madman a nail and you puncture the horizon budget, you come up with your proposal. you cut eur 180 million off the proposal on digital europe. at the same time, you’re selling to the international audience a chip programme; you’re selling that. you promised already. you want to have 3.3 billion for that budget, and rightly so. it is one of europe’s ambitions. but at the same time, you cut the digital budget before we even started the discussion of where and how to finance new ambitions. so, let’s be realistic. either you say ‘no’ to innovation in europe, or we should start to negotiate.
adopted on 16 february. the commission has decided to extend the period of validity of the authorisation for the placing on the market of the medicinal product concerned to the period from 1 january 2002 to 31 december 2003. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council. the opinion of the committee on the internal market is, as always, relatively balanced, because we do earn a good deal of the money that the member states and the commission spend. it was therefore not a big problem to agree on the opinion here, but i would like to highlight two issues which are of particular importance to the colleagues on the committee on the internal market. we know that in the budgetary procedure we as members of parliament defend our budget, the commission defends its budget and the council tries to pay as little as possible. we respect that. but the legislation on the digital markets act and the digital services act is about us creating a new regulatory unit across europe that will allow us to work together not only to better enforce the principle of the social market economy, but also to provide revenue to the commission and to reduce the responsibilities of the member states. that is why we are all calling - and i am only one of many here as rapporteur for the committee on the internal market - for the european commission to be given new staff in the area where it is taking over responsibilities from the member states, staff that is no longer needed by the member states. parliament has only regulatory interests and is somewhat supportive of the european commission's idea. we know that the budgetary procedure is not so simple, and i would therefore like to thank the colleagues who are responsible for this procedure, niclas herbst and mr lewandowski, for their support and i hope that we will have a serious discussion of this matter.

rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on women’s rights and gender equality. – madam president, commissioner, council, on behalf of the committee on women’s rights and gender equality (femm), i would very much like to thank the rapporteurs and the budget committee for their very good work. as was already mentioned, the circumstances for this year’s budgetary procedure are very challenging, and i very much acknowledge the efforts to achieve a very good compromise. the femm committee delivered a strong opinion, and i am glad that some crucial elements are reflected in the parliament’s overall amendments in the budgetary resolution. first of all, the proposed increase of the daphne strand by eur 2 million above the draft budget is a clear acknowledgement of the importance of tackling the alarming problem of gender based violence. although this is considerably less than our committee would have liked to see, it is a crucial signal to increase the funding for the daphne strand. secondly, the very good wording regarding the commission’s efforts for a methodology to measure the gender impact of the union’s funding. the budgetary resolution includes concrete demands on measures, on scope and on gender disaggregated data. this type of scrutiny is very important, and parliament’s strong stance on the eu4 health programme and the inclusion of our committee’s perspective that universal health coverage across the union needs to include quality access to sexual and reproductive health and rights is necessary to involve. lastly, specific support for equality for civil society organisations defending women’s rights and the rights of the lgbtiq community under heading six ‘neighbourhood and the world’ is very important, and i would like to stress that this will be crucial to defend in the upcoming negotiations.

in writing. on 14 november, world diabetes day was celebrated under the theme 'education to protect tomorrow'. in europe, it is estimated that 1 in 3 people with diabetes will not be diagnosed, and the prevalence of this disease continues to increase. diabetes has a very significant impact on people's lives and, if not properly monitored and pharmacologically controlled, can lead to blindness, limb amputation, and kidney failure, among other comorbidities. it's currently the leading preventable cause of these disabling complications. the european union has an added moral obligation in defining public health policies for prevention, but also for disease management. effective treatment requires access to insulin and other oral medicines used to manage type 2 diabetes and prevent complications, as well as relevant lifestyle interventions, health education, self-management of therapy, and access to devices and technologies. these are measures which bring very significant benefits to the health of citizens.

the next item is the debate on the recommendation for second reading by the committee on legal affairs and the committee on women's rights and gender equality on the council's position at first reading with a view to the adoption of a directive of the european parliament and of the council on improving the representation of women in boards of listed companies and related measures (10521/1/2022 - c9-0354/2022 - 2012/0299 ((cod)) (rapporteurs: lara wolters and evelyn regner) (a9-0275/2022).
the rapporteur. the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat fraud. thank you, lara, for the great cooperation. this directive is obsolete. this directive that is coming now is the teamwork of many great people, especially women, of course, across party lines, across institutional lines and - we can of course also say this - across legislative periods. if it's about creating more equality, i would say, more normality, which is what we want to see in business, then obviously we need to take a very long breath, because it's always about power. for ten years we in the european parliament have kept the dossier alive. for ten years, as rapporteur, i have been fighting from the beginning, together with many others in this room and beyond, to finally abolish the male quota in supervisory boards. and now we've finally made it. this directive on more women in management positions means that qualifications are finally given top priority in supervisory boards and management boards. the targets are to be met by june 30, 2026, and with that, we will finally be able to better map society, create diversity, improve the corporate culture for everyone from the top down. the directive focuses on the procedure for the selection of members of the supervisory board and also of the management board. if the selection procedure is flawed, the best qualified candidates often do not even make it to the final round, to the detriment of both the company and the candidates. the directive therefore emphasizes transparency and efficiency in the selection process. especially when it's tough economically -- with the pandemic, with putin's aggression in ukraine, with inflation and the climate crisis -- we need resilient, strong companies in the european union, and we're only going to get there if those companies finally get women on board and driving. if we harness the full potential of smart minds and create transparent procurement processes, then of course, at the end of the day, the companies themselves benefit. because they get the best qualified people. that's what this is about. and those companies that meet the targets will also be put on a "faming" list, as a positive example for everyone. but we also know that without binding measures nothing will happen. the commission has also taken into account the fact that the commission has not yet adopted a decision on the application of the individual quantitative targets for the period from 1 january 2014 to 31 december 2015. this can be a fine, a revocation of the election as well as restrictions on public procurement depending on what works best where. of course, this also includes transparently reporting what the figures are, taking good action and having an authority check this too. we have also included this in this directive. the understanding of the directive is clear: more women in decision-making positions - this is just one of many measures that must be taken to achieve gender equality in the workplace. nevertheless, it is an important part, an important component of this mosaic. it is high time for positive change, for modern enterprises and, above all, for equality for women in all areas of the economy. that is why we, as a team in the european parliament, are more than proud that we have finally achieved this after ten years. and so, really, my big thanks to all these wonderful shadow reporters, to this great team that just stuck together in this regard. i would like to thank commissioner helena dalli for her incredibly convincing report. i would also like to thank the representatives of several presidencies - the french presidency before, the czech one now, of course, and the luxembourg and maltese ones in the past. i say this especially now because we've really had to stick together here over so much time. and it turns out, it pays off. so a big day, i guess, for all of us.
member of the commission. – mr president, honourable members, thank you, lara wolters, and thank you, evelyn regner. you really captured the spirit of this legislation this morning when you say that the girls cannot be what they cannot see. and it’s exactly that. after ten years of negotiations, the directive on gender balance on corporate boards is finally becoming european law. with the adoption of the directive, we will empower women to reach their full potential, move to a new chapter of eu gender equality policy, and, again, girls will now start trying to be what they can see. so we will turn that that statement over its head, hopefully with this. europe has many, as we’ve heard, qualified women, as 60 % of current university graduates are female. nevertheless, this investment in women’s education and training is not mirrored in the positions they hold in decision-making, as women are still underrepresented in high positions, including, of course, on corporate boards. this is a clear waste of human capital. we have made progress in the past, but not fast enough. and, as we know, not everywhere in the european union. the gender equality index, published every year by the european institute for gender equality, shows that at a time at the time of the proposal, women represented only 12 % of board members across the eu, compared to 31.6 % today. and the share of women in business management across the eu at the national level ranges from 8 % to 46 %. so data also shows that member states which introduce binding measures, like gender equality targets, made the biggest progress. while the advancement triggered by voluntary initiatives was much slower and less sustainable. so when self-regulation does not bring the desired effect, eu regulatory action is necessary. and this is precisely why this directive is so urgently needed. so the directive addresses the lack of transparency in the selection process and the lack of objective qualification criteria which constitute the main obstacle to achieving gender-balanced representation. in this way, the directive ensures that the selection of board members is gender neutral and merit based. the directive should not be seen as a burden on companies but as a tool to bring concrete benefits to european and national economies. evidence shows that a broad range of talents and skills and an inclusive and diverse approach in the boardroom contribute to better decisions and corporate governance, increase public trust in business and drive economic growth. and this is particularly important in the current turbulent economic situation. we need all hands on deck to recover and reboost our economies and achieve a union of equality. thank you for your hard work.
on behalf of the ppe group. madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, today we are making history by making the women on boards directive a reality, after a decade of insistence from the european parliament. we sent a message to the future. a message to men and women, because the goal is to protect the most underrepresented gender. but a message, we know well, aimed primarily at young women. to these we say: believe me, your talent, your commitment, will be the deciding factors in your rise to the top. in large companies, which is what is now at stake, and in all other dimensions of society. today, women represent some 60% of new higher education graduates in the european union, half of the workforce, but only 31.5% of board members and 8% of chairpersons. when they say that this directive must ensure respect for merit, i say: i agree. that is precisely the ambition of the directive. and this is a step that will benefit all europeans, because we cannot waste talent. we need the best, men and women, in decision-making positions. thank you very much to all those who have not given up over the last ten years, and a very special word of thanks to the initiator of the process, viviane reding, then vice-president of the commission.
on behalf of the ecr group. madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, we are talking about 40% of non-executive posts, 33% if we include executives. what's there to celebrate? the presence of women certainly enriches and improves the results. we celebrate female talent, pride in our nature and the nuances we can bring to decision-making. but this directive even puts a limit on the presence of women. to present ourselves as helpless and naturally disadvantaged does not help if what we want is to show the value of women. freedom of enterprise is under attack. it is up to an organisation to decide on the composition of its board. it is a political interference in companies, with a regulation which should be legal and commercial, in accordance with national, not european, legislation. quotas are a drastic, discriminatory and unfair system. the never-ending story. now by sex, well, no, by gender. but it could also be because of race or ethnicity. it's disrespectful to look at people's characteristics and not at people. it is our training or abilities or experience that should determine us. collectivization makes us one more of a group. it takes away our names and surnames, confronts us and accentuates prejudice. it turns just causes into power struggles. let us restore hope and harmony to this world. measures like this only increase the cramping.
the commission has already made a number of proposals. equality is an endless story, unfortunately. twelve years ago, our former epp commissioner viviane reding launched the women on board directive, and thus laid the foundations for legislation on gender equality in business in many member states. equal rights for men and women are still not a given today. there are still more men than women on the boards, and if you look at the boardroom floor, there is very little air, there is very little control over the number of women on the board. quotas are a door opener. i think they make sense in breaking down long-standing, decades-old structures, and that is why it is good that we are finally adopting this directive today. but what is important, besides the quotas, is that there is the right mindset in the companies. i would like to thank the rapporteur for his excellent report, which i am sure will be of great help to the commission. let me also point out that i am deeply convinced that if we have a balanced representation of women and men in management positions in companies, this will also be a win for many companies: many studies suggest that a significant representation of women in management positions can also be a success factor for company sales, profits. i am glad that this directive is being adopted today.
madam president, five plus five: it has been ten years since we had to wait for an agreement to improve the balance between men and women on the boards of publicly traded companies. how many more must we wait for this balance to become a reality, not just in publicly traded companies but in all of them, as something natural, effortless, part of life on a planet where men and women share equally? women account for 60% of new higher education graduates in the european union, but only 8% of board presidents are women. this was a great moment for all of us and a struggle that the socialists, in particular, did not let die. a special thanks to colleagues evelyn regner and lara wolters. we should be proud of this step, but we cannot ignore the feeling that we are always running late, always chasing the problem and never in time to avoid it.
mr president, can we create the ideal world with laws and regulations? many meps seem to think so. the european union wants to take the world into its own hands through legislation and regulation, through anti-money laundering legislation, sustainability rules such as the csddd and gender provisions. to make the world your oyster with a deluge of laws and regulations is impossible and dangerous. everyone knows that as politics becomes more involved in business, the boundaries between the public and the private sector are blurred. more red tape. more management and control. less freedom. less wealth. an irreversible process that leads to impoverishment. can we create an ideal world with laws and regulations? seneca said no. he said: the road to regulations is long, the road to examples is short and focused. (the president declared the motion for a resolution adopted)
blue card reply. i'm here to stop the glut of legislation and regulation among other things. i know from good examples in the united states, for example, that it is very possible to achieve your goals without a plethora of laws and regulations, but that requires innovation. i'm for a world that's safe. i am against human trafficking, i am against forced labor and i am a great supporter of human rights. but i'm not a believer in rules and regulations.
madam president, commissioner, dear colleagues, it is a sad fact that only 30.6 % of board members in our largest companies are women, knowing that girls do better in schools than boys and knowing that in the european union 60 % of new graduates from universities are women. many studies show that gender quotas are the most effective instrument for increasing women’s presence on company boards. voluntary regulations without sanctions does not work. therefore we socialists and democrats are pleased that we finally, after 10 years, adopted this women on boards directive. this directive will improve gender equality by ensuring that at least 40 % of non-executive director posts or 30 % of all director posts are occupied by the underrepresented sex and this by 2026 through transparent, clear and neutral appointment criteria. this directive also rightly foresees dissuasive penalties for non-compliance and it’s a great success for us socialists and democrats. our fight against gender inequalities can no longer wait and it will go on. i call on more men, i call on all men to speak up for gender equality and fight the patriarchal model that subsists in our society. gender equality is a recipe for prosperity. this directive is not only good for women, it is good for the progress, the sustainability and the success of our european companies and economy.
madam president, this directive has been blocked for ten years and during these ten years, seven out of ten board members of listed companies have been men and less than one in ten of the large companies in the european union have had a female president or chief executive officer. the directive was therefore absolutely necessary and urgently needed to strike a balance in these boards of directors of listed companies, where the majority of the positions are held by men, not because they are the best, but because in the selection process gender has been a criterion and women have been discriminated against. so today we also send a message of hope to the young women who are in universities, in colleges. from now on, this directive ensures a fair and transparent selection process, with criteria based on neutrality, where gender is not going to be a discriminatory element. from now on, at least 40% will be women, but there may be more because there will be capacity criteria. just tell them that ten years show the difficulties in making progress on equality. and i think today is also a time to thank all the women in this house, from all the political groups, who have kept the flame and the strength going for this great day to come.
blue-card speech. – dear colleague, you said the ‘numbers are important’. so since you consider gender something as the same importance as sex, i suppose that you think that also certain numbers of lgbtq people should be ceos there. and also, since we don’t want to be racist, also certain numbers of different skin colours should be in these committees. so my question is: do you intend to implement quotas also and to impose quotas to firms concerning lgbtq and races?
madam president, coming from the financial sector, one of the companies i worked for was looking for a female director. and after a long and proactive recruitment procedure they couldn’t find a candidate. and it wasn’t just the board. the company proactively recruited women. still, men remained overrepresented. more than three quarters of companies in the financial sector are actively trying to attract female talent. but 64% of them do not see any progress in the number of female applicants. when we compare this to other sectors, for example medicine, we see women on the rise without quotas. in the netherlands, approximately 66% of all doctors and 55% of specialist doctors will soon be women. women achieve excellence in their chosen careers. we should recognise that there are gender-specific preferences to specific types of work. let us give individuals and businesses the freedom to decide what they want and what works best for themselves.
