blue-card speech. – you mentioned those enterprises who would like to have more women there in top positions. when you talked to those, i mean guys leading the enterprises, did you raise also the question of the culture of how the enterprises are led? i had talks with many of those in top positions and then got information: it’s about the culture, it’s about how the atmosphere is on boards and that many top women, meanwhile, already say, i expect that there is a change, that there is respect, there is another form of treat, how to treat each other concerning working hours and everything else. so did you take that on board with these considerations you just mentioned?
blue-card reply. – thank you for these questions, and i will try to answer them and i will try to condense them into one answer. the example i gave on medicine, i think it clearly shows that it is possible for women to choose a career and to make a career in a specific type of environment they would like to seek to make their career. but if you look at quotas, if you look at the rationale or the ideas behind quotas, it says we need more female ideas because women bring other ideas. but then the question i’m asking is: what are female ideas? isn’t this a very contradicting way? and isn’t this a very contradicting thing to say? i mean, i’m all for diversity of ideas, but that should be based on character, not on sex, race or ethnicity. so i think that’s the bottom line.
madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, today we are taking a huge step forward for gender equality, for women's rights. this is a significant moment, important of course for us, but it is important above all, i believe, for future generations. and we are making this very important step forward - let me say this - thanks to the tenacity of this parliament, thanks to the tenacity of the commissioner, thanks to the colleagues who have worked tirelessly on this directive - i am looking at evelyn - because for ten years some governments have held us absolutely hostage, have been short-sighted in the face of progress, in the face of truth, in the face of justice, have blocked common sense rules that they had and that have the ambition simply to correct a distortion of reality that is the child of stereotypes and an unacceptable patriarchal culture. we are therefore taking a partial step forward, madam president, and i conclude in an area which is crucial for the future of all women.
madam president, commissioner, this is a landmark directive and it’s very welcome. and as we’ve heard many times, there is vast research and evidence which demonstrates the economic value of increasing representation on boards. in ireland we have made great progress over the last very short period of time. irish plcs already have 32% female representation on boards. ireland also leads the way with women in top jobs. a survey of 24 countries showed that ireland actually leads these countries – including the uk, the us, canada, australia and singapore. and perhaps it’s no coincidence, despite the dire warnings we’ve heard about the performance of the economy, that ireland is the leading economy in the european union over the last couple of years. and i wanted to pay tribute to the rapporteurs and everybody that has been involved in this, but also to renew europe, because it was a renew europe priority for this parliamentary term. i want to pay tribute to my colleagues, samira rafaela and to karen melchior. and before i finish, i want to say we should focus as well on representation in politics. it’s important that we do this in economics, but some of the most important boards in the world are governments. and just look at the last g20, where there were just two female leaders present among the 20.

blue-card reply. – the whole debate about having to quota people in is not doing anyone a favour who will be quota—ed in. because the problem is that if someone gets quota—ed in for a position – and it doesn’t matter what kind of thing they are being quota—ed in for – the other people around will think they are there because of the quota, not because of the competence. that’s the biggest problem. i know from my class when i was studying, from my university classes, that it was over—represented with women, and i can see that many of them now have good positions. it takes time for this to happen, and i believe that it’s going to go through and also that if you have two people and they have the same competence you will choose the person who is right. i believe that is true and it’s going to be more and more that women will be over—represented in many areas, like my colleague said, in the public sector. it will be like that.

president-in-office of the council. – mr president, it’s my pleasure to attend today the european parliament’s debate on the general budget of the european union for the financial year 2023. i would like to express the council’s satisfaction that we were able to reach an agreement in the conciliation committee in the very last minutes of 14 november, the last day of the conciliation period, after long and intense discussions. the positive outcome of the negotiations shows that our three institutions can work efficiently in a spirit of good cooperation. together, we negotiated a budget for 2023, which responds to the concerns of today and to the requirements of tomorrow of european citizens. the european parliament and the council have worked hard to find a solution that would equip the union for the challenges of the third year of the multiannual financial framework. the deal we have reached reflects our common priorities, in particular addressing the consequences of the war in ukraine, the severe energy crisis combined with the historically high level of inflation, the disruption of global supply lines and the enduring post-pandemic recovery, all in addition to fighting climate change and fostering the eu’s green and digital transition. i will not hide from you that the council would have preferred more flexibility in the budget to allow the union to promptly react to unexpected events that we may face in these unpredictable times. in the official meetings in the course of the year, as well as during informal contacts, the council appreciated very much the positive atmosphere that prevailed in these talks. both sides of the table were ready to engage constructively to reach an agreement, to reframe the discussion around the shared interests and come up with solutions. i am glad to inform you that after the successful outcome of the conciliation committee on 14 november, the joint text was formally approved by the council earlier today by a qualified majority. the council expects the european parliament to also approve the joint text tomorrow. the general budget for the financial year 2023 will then be adopted within the deadlines foreseen by the treaty. coming to the end of my intervention. i would like to thank the chairman of the european parliament’s committee on budgets, johan van overtveldt, and the rapporteurs, nicolae ștefănuță and niclas herbst, for the good cooperation during the last weeks and months. i would also like to extend my special thanks to you, commissioner hahn, for the way you facilitated the negotiations.
member of the commission. – mr president, dear minister, honourable members and, in particular, dear rapporteurs, of course i welcome the agreement on the european union annual budget 2023 reached by the conciliation committee on monday 14 november, as usual just before midnight. this is not really unexpected, but let me also say, for the protocol, that i very much welcome the fact that we have this debate at a very prominent time. but i have to say that it’s for the second time in a row that i am missing a college meeting, because nowadays parliament is debating at the time when the college meeting is taking place, and i think that’s something i would really like to reconsider for the future anyway. i think the fact that we got an agreement is our shared success. this was already expressed by the previous speakers. in such unprecedented times, it was more than ever important to put aside our differences and to work together on reaching our shared goals and priorities. the initial positions were, like always, quite far apart – a couple of billion euros – but finally it was possible to reconcile them. i appreciate that all parties acted responsibly and made the necessary compromises in order to have finally a solid agreement on time. i would like to thank the european parliament, especially the committee on budgets and all the specialised committees, for the intensive work on the commission’s proposal for the draft budget 2023. this is a half—year work which starts usually in early july. on behalf of the commission, i really would like to thank, in particular, the chair, mr van overtveldt, and the two rapporteurs, mr ştefănuță and mr herbst, for their cooperation. i would also like to thank the czech presidency – and please convey thanks to the minister georgiev for his constructive approach during these negotiations. it also shows that the institutions can work towards a compromise for the benefit of the european union and its citizens. for this, again, congratulations to the negotiators and the teams. i would also like to include in this my own team, because they have done a great job. for johan ureel, who is with me today, this will be his final budget. he was our ‘mr budget’. he made seven annual budgets and before that he was in charge of the mff for 14 years. so johan, thank you very much for your impressive job. let me now give you a few details on the agreement. the overall level of commitment appropriations in next year’s budget is set at eur 186.6 billion. the overall level of payment appropriations in the 2023 budget is set at eur 168.7 billion. the rapporteurs have already explained the reinforcements for parliament’s priorities, with emphasis on ukraine, moldova, tackling the energy crisis, building solidarity lanes, strengthening erasmus+, life, smes, eu4health, creative europe, rights and values programmes, as well as the european solidarity corps, reinforcing migration funds, providing funds for military mobility, supporting the eastern and southern neighbourhood, the key global europe programme and strengthening the asylum agency and eu—lisa – just to list a few agreed reinforcements. but the annual budget also includes a package of 39 pilot projects and preparatory actions for a total amount of eur 80.1 billion as proposed by parliament. once adopted, the annual budget 2023 would allow the union to mobilise significant funds to help mitigate the severe consequences of russia’s war of aggression against ukraine, not only in the country but also in the member states and in the neighbourhood. it would also support the ongoing sustainable recovery from the coronavirus pandemic by maintaining and creating jobs. it would trigger further investments into a greener, more digital and more resilient europe while protecting the most vulnerable in the european union, its neighbourhood and around the world. next year’s budget will direct funds to where they can make the greatest difference, in line with the most crucial needs of the member states and the union’s partners around the world. looking forward, let me recall that we are almost halfway through the long—term budget. with the numerous unprecedented developments and challenges it has become even more clear that we should focus on a limited number of important issues to maximise the effectiveness of our funding, with targeted reinforcements concentrated on a limited number of issues with a real impact. in that respect, i would like to thank the rapporteurs again because there was some progress compared to the previous budget, but i think even more focusing and targeted reinforcements could be possible. i also reiterate my strong plea for all institutions to exercise restraint in administrative expenditure and to keep stable staff numbers in order not to increase the heavy pressure on heading 7. the reality is that we cannot continue adding new posts for institutions and respect the ceiling and sub—ceilings in heading 7. going forward, we will have to face some tough choices between what we would like to do and what we can do. the key word is negative priorities. i can assure you that the commission will focus its future proposal, including the upcoming draft budget 2024, on the most effective and efficient use of our limited resources, to deliver on our citizens expectations and needs.
on behalf of the ppe group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your kind words. budgetary agreements are always good news in these troubled times, with so much uncertainty. this is the wartime budget. the macro-financial assistance does not have direct budgetary implications, but it contains elements of assistance to ukraine, primarily in the neighbourhood policy. it's an inflationary budget, which makes the projects expensive. this is a budget of high interest rates, which increases the cost of running the european recovery instrument. the small margins forced the use of special instruments: the flexibility and single margin instruments. the european parliament increased funding for its priorities by €687 million. our rapporteur, of course, because that is also his, first and foremost, merit nicolae ştefănuţă counts, and rightly so, the commitments. the fact that both sides came out of the negotiations satisfied is a measure of the creativity of the european commission under commissioner hahn. the priorities are very well defined, including the political priorities of my group: horizon here we use article 15 of the financial regulation, connectivity energy and transport, small and medium-sized enterprises, eu health, creative europe, clean energy transition and the eastern partnership. most importantly, in these turbulent times, by providing financial certainty, we have made a modest contribution to reducing the uncertainty and fears about the future that have returned to our continent.
on behalf of the s&d group. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, we have succeeded, we have saved the european budget for next year from austerity policy and from those who, in the current difficult context, want to jeopardise the european construction with a budget that is less than a living wage. if we want a more united and stronger european union, that provides adequate responses to the war in ukraine and to managing the effects of the pandemic, that truly protects european citizens in the context of runaway inflation and the energy crisis, or that lives up to our common expectations, we need a more ambitious budget. but unfortunately, not all decision-makers understand this. we negotiated until the last minute to protect european programmes and policies that are essential for our citizens in the current context, we managed to get additional funds to combat rising energy prices for the transport sector, for smes, for education, for young people, for the environment or for culture. this budget also provides for additional resources for the republic of moldova and ukraine, as well as for the accession of romania, bulgaria and croatia to the schengen area. to ensure the resources needed to implement our common ambitions, i call for a review of the european budget in the long term.

on behalf of the s&d group. mr president, commissioner, the free movement of persons in the european union is a right of all children, including those who are the children of rainbow families. but as the case of the baby sara, already decided by the court of justice, shows, the authorities of certain countries refuse to recognise parental relationship of children of same-sex couples, even when it has already been recognised in another member state. this creates enormous problems for the lives of these families and seriously affects these children. parents may, for example, be prevented from accompanying their children to a hospital or even from allowing them to go on a simple school excursion. in extreme cases, if one of the spouses dies in a member state which does not recognise the parental relationship, the child may lose not one but both parental figures. the legislative proposal on the crossborder recognition of parentage, which i hope the commission will present shortly, should ensure the protection of the freedom of movement of children from these families, ensuring that parental relationships and the rights associated with them are recognised for all and respected throughout the union. in the european union, no child can lose his or her rights simply by moving from one member state to another.

on behalf of the ppe group. madam president, cop's come and go, but results tend to be scarce and time is running out. here in europe we have been presenting results for several years, and we have taken our homework to successive cop's. at the same time that we've increased our productivity and our gdp, we've been able to reduce our emissions. here, we invest in innovation, in new technologies, in renewables and we demand more from our companies. we can do more, like in electricity interconnections and the energy union. but in this cop27 we have seen the lack of results on the most important of the issues - and the vice-president of the commission has just mentioned it - namely the reduction of emissions. ladies and gentlemen, this is where we really need to have results to prevent the floods, fires and storms we have all experienced from getting worse. we see and hear beautiful speeches and motives everywhere, but few commitments and very few results. what is the point of funding the poorest countries to combat the effects of climate change if the most polluting countries continue to pollute and exacerbate the problem? in europe, we have shown leadership in raising the 2030 targets for reducing emissions. we are the main funder of the fight against climate change in developing countries, but we have not been able to have a mobilizing agenda with the biggest polluters: china, the united states and india. our climate diplomacy has not been producing the necessary results and this is an important reflection that we must make without delay with the commission and the council.

madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, for too long rural areas have been left behind, with our best talent moving away for education and employment opportunities. for the first time we have a chance to turn this around. we can embrace a new generation of opportunities, whether it’s remote working that can bring highly skilled people back into rural areas and let them rear a family and breathe life into rural communities, or tourism that can bring much—needed revenue into rural areas. but most importantly, renewable energies can bring industry and employment and rebalance the economic driver back into our rural areas. but we must ensure that the revenue from these new opportunities remains in rural economies and drives the rural economies and is not exported out to other areas. we need to create the right environment. we need broadband. we need to reinvigorate our town centres to make them vibrant places for people to live. we need to create the educational opportunities for people in rural areas, the very same as there are available in urban areas. we also have to acknowledge that agriculture has always been and remains a cornerstone of rural communities. it’s the biggest multiplier in the rural economies. it’s the heart of our food production system and is central to protecting our biodiversity and building a sustainable environment into the future.
madam president, we must not forget the countryside for too long. in the european parliament, we deal with regions, cities and countryside as if it were an imaginary cinderella. it's not that we don't care, but it's like cohesion policy and its results don't reach the countryside. and that's a great pity. cohesion policy is, of course, intended to reduce disparities between regions and must primarily help to keep the countryside alive, a countryside that is truly full of farmers, full of jobs, full of young people. and yet, it seems that cities are acting like magnets, drawing in jobs, people, technology, services. so what we have to focus on is the availability of infrastructure and services in the countryside. that's for sure. secondly, the weakening of the cohesion envelope of money earmarked for regional aid must stop. we must focus on supporting the countryside, including by maintaining the rations that are supposed to be provided.
we believe, madam president, that this report makes a fair diagnosis of the reality of the rural world in some member states and that it has a strong focus on agriculture as the main activity in rural areas. we follow many of the concerns expressed. we are in favour of the development of the incomes of small and mediumsized farmers in order to maintain activity and to develop rural areas, aspects which the common agricultural policy and its successive reforms have neglected by unfairly distributing aid and favouring agribusiness. the report could have gone further in defending the social functions of the state and public services in health, education and care for children, the elderly and disabled people, with infrastructures covering the whole of the territories, giving high quality and proximity responses. if we continue to follow the same old policies, which are moving away from the path of cohesion, and if we insist on making people yield to the taste of big capital, we may even have stronger, more connected and more prosperous rural areas, but it will not be to the benefit of the people, but to the pockets of a few.
rural areas account for 83% of the total european territory and are home to some 137 million people, a significant proportion of the european population. these areas face a number of challenges, such as isolation, ageism and excessive rural exodus, which then translates into lack of opportunities, inequalities, and lack of access to services and goods, often exacerbated when connected to other factors, such as the outermost periphery or difficult terrain. this justifies the interest and importance of drawing attention to a response that attempts to address these difficulties, including through the recognition of their enormous potential in areas of major importance: food and agricultural production, renewable energy production, protection of biodiversity, among others. investment in these areas will promote the attractiveness of the areas for employment and tourism, with very positive social repercussions. this requires the involvement of regional and local authorities, who will be better aware of the specific characteristics of the regions, and increased funding, in particular through the cohesion funds. the development of these areas along this path responds to present and future challenges that ensure social and territorial cohesion, combat depopulation and are in line with the objectives of protecting the planet we pursue.
the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area. we agree on many things: we need to accelerate to meet the renewable targets - no question. we must grant permits more quickly, renewables must be given priority - clearly, we agree. and we need to prioritize wind and solar in particular. these are our greatest potentials. but we must not forget, commissioner, that the european union is not the only one. you brought it up. we also need to talk about energy efficiency, renewables and savings. and we're missing that in a lot of ways. we still have problems and i am still very critical of biomass. we cannot increase the rate of wood biomass combustion any further. in the case of biogas, we have to recycle waste, obviously, and not rely on building corn power plants. that's not the future. unfortunately, the epp wants to eliminate a few environmental laws in this whole legislation. they'll have to expect our resistance. and let me be clear: natura 2000 sites can and must have special protection. dear markus pieper: it is not only environmental associations that are hindering the expansion, but also excessive distance regulations in some federal states such as bavaria.
i have to say, madam president, the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for approval. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject. and in this context i also believe that we should evaluate the sanctions against russia in general. because the only effect that these sanctions are having at the moment is that we here in europe and the people in europe are feeling that energy is getting more and more expensive and soon it will no longer be affordable. the failure of energy policy in recent decades has led to the fact that people are no longer able to heat their homes, and that we, for example, are no longer able to heat our three sites, which we so badly need. the only thing that is making this house a little hotter is the s&d group's potentially incendiary contacts with qatar, which are also illegal. but one thing remains: we will not be able to meet the energy demand for 2030, 2035, when we know that electric mobility will increase dramatically, with wind turbines.
madam president, since the signing of the versailles declaration, we have intensified our work in the european parliament, and in particular in the itre committee, to enable the union to act as quickly as possible in implementing the repower eu plan and adapting the fit for 55 proposals to this plan. the directive on the promotion of the use of energy from renewable sources has already been amended twice. as chair of the itre committee, i would like to thank and congratulate my colleague markus piper and the colleagues responsible from the political groups for the rapid reaction and team spirit over the last few months. we will therefore support the acceleration of the authorisation procedures for renewable energy projects, a better definition of access areas, criteria for the establishment of sites of public interest. in parallel to our legislative work, the european commission has proposed a new temporary council emergency regulation to accelerate the implementation of renewable energy production under article 122. and even though we have serious reservations about the far too frequent use of article 122, the european parliament has taken account of and is coordinating its position with this commission initiative. ladies and gentlemen, mr executive vice-president, parliament has shown that it can react quickly and in full solidarity with the other institutions. we firmly assure european citizens that we will work effectively with member states, with the support of the commission, to have the best legislation, to find solutions to the current price crisis and to achieve our ambitious climate targets.
the commission has already made a number of proposals. the european parliament, together with the member states, is serious about developing renewable energy. if we reach 45% renewable by 2030, we can save 200 billion euros that we don't have to invest in fossil fuels. we can make ourselves independent of putin's gas. and that goal is attainable with a united effort. i am glad that we have discussed and agreed broadly here in the house, but also in the council, on the simplification of procedures which we need in order to have more renewable energy and to develop it more rapidly. we have an agreement that provides for proportionate development while respecting nature conservation law. the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for approval. no more environmental audits for biomass power plants: it's dangerous, it doesn't make sense and it doesn't help anyone. the commission is also proposing to increase the number of natura 2000 sites. it would be much more helpful in the states to abolish unnecessary renewable waste land in federal states such as bavaria. that is why we must stick to the compromise and vote on it today.
madam president, europe is undoubtedly in need of a complete transformation of its energy system, which requires key decisions on energy policies, the pace of decisions on an efficient energy transition and energy independence. the war in ukraine has shown us in no uncertain terms how important this is for the future of europe. the implementation of repowereu will accelerate the deployment of renewable energy sources in europe, encourage further enhancement of energy efficiency as an important component of the whole system and ultimately lead to increased energy savings, energy security and independence. the proposed changes include enhanced measures to speed up the permitting procedures for new res and solar installations. it is a necessary measure, which we support and which will be effective if the member states are actively involved in its implementation and if we find the support of local authorities.
madam president, good morning mr executive vice—president. yesterday, a historic scientific breakthrough was announced. in ten years, nuclear fusion might power a completely decarbonised energy system. but we are still talking about ten years at least. what do we do until then? shall we keep growing our dependence on fossil fuels and foreign countries that produce them? doesn’t seem very wise. shall we keep pouring water on conventional nuclear plants and sweep their waste under the rug? the solution in the meanwhile is investing in solar panels, wind turbines, heat pumps. this is the cheapest and fastest way to decarbonise the energy system now. and we need to accelerate because the climate crisis does not wait for administrative procedures to be done. citizens are on our side, on this as long as we let them be part of the process and aid their advantage on the process, on bills, for example, and don’t impose choices on them. citizens are actually the power that our renewable energy system is built on.
madam president, this strategic plan proposes to intervene in the energy system, reacting to developments in this sector since last year, aggravated by the consequences of war and sanctions which, in accordance with the agenda of the united states of america, are sacrificing the peoples of europe. the measures envisaged in their various forms are essentially in the interests of the major economic groups and european powers. the path traced is far from meeting the needs of the people, ensuring socioeconomic equity and respecting the sovereignty of the states. as in the past, household consumers will bear the cost of the so-called single energy market, while the large economic groups, which exploit the sector and resources, rub their hands and accumulate profits. we believe that it is necessary for the states to intervene in the exploration, supply, production, transport and marketing of the various forms of energy, which should be in the public domain, under public and democratic control. it is in this context that it is urgent to provide the necessary answers to the families and small businesses facing serious difficulties. this is the socially and environmentally sustainable way.
the child guarantee was set up to respond to the shameful situation of having millions of children living in poverty in one of the richest territories in the world, europe, without access to adequate food and basic public services. the programme is important and well designed, but it lacks the resources it needs. it makes no sense, and it cannot continue to happen, for the european commission to announce new programmes, however fair, to require member states to respond to humanitarian crises, such as the refugee crisis in ukraine and, for that matter, also the other refugee crises that have been forgotten, without presenting new resources for these priorities. we cannot have new priorities and respond to new emergencies with the same money as always or, in the case of the european social fund+, with less money than existed in previous cycles. so if we really want to meet this demand that is being made of us, this very just demand, we need new resources, additional resources.
mr president, the child guarantee is an important instrument for immediate responses. about 25% of children in the european union are at risk of poverty and social exclusion. its application in the member states already allows conclusions to be drawn. their means are not even sufficient to compensate for the loss of family incomes due to the surge in inflation, the consequences of the war and the disastrous sanctions policy of the european union. for the child guarantee to have a real impact, a significant increase in its budget is essential. but fighting child poverty is done with structural policies, not with patches. the guarantee that goes beyond appearance is the guarantee of universal, free and quality education, including at the level of early childhood, the guarantee of universal, free and quality health services, the guarantee that parents and carers have a job with rights, decent pay and time for children, the guarantee of the right to play, to sport, to culture, to civic participation, the guarantee of living in a world of peace. i would like to end with a quote from nelson mandela: history will judge us by the difference we make in the day-to-day lives of children.

mr president, commissioner, the seriousness of the allegations of corruption and the facts that have already been revealed are a deep stain on this institution and the european institutions. for my part, disgust was what i felt in the first moments, angry and more and more angry, with every new revelation, with every new fact that we come to know. i am proud that my political group has already acted, and acted immediately, as this parliament has done, by expelling eva kaili from our political group and removing her from the post of vice-president of parliament. but we have much more to do to restore citizens' confidence in our institution and in the european institutions. we must also act in the context of the judicial process, because we have also been a party to the damage, if these acts are confirmed, by setting up a committee of inquiry to look into the situation in depth, but by taking concrete measures to protect the institution against further attacks of the same nature. we must preserve the integrity of european democracy, because, as mrs sophia in 't veld said here a moment ago, it takes a long time to build up this credibility, but it can be lost in a moment. let's work to change this situation.
mr president, commissioner, it is almost three decades since the signing of the oslo accords created hope for a peaceful solution for israel and palestine and their peoples. a solution based on two sovereign states, israel and palestine, peacefully coexisting, with jerusalem as the capital of both states. unfortunately, many steps have been taken since then in the wrong direction, making it increasingly difficult to implement this solution. the consequences of these bad choices have been paid for with insecurity, with lives, with underdevelopment. with this resolution, the european parliament is sending a very clear message to all those involved in the process, including the new israeli government, the far-right party that will be part of it, the various radical parties that will be part of it and, in particular, some of the ministers in this new government. it is necessary to resume peace negotiations leading to the implementation of the two-state solution in accordance with international law and un resolutions. we strongly condemn all systematic actions that undermine the settlement of this conflict, including violence by either side, or the occupation of land or the establishment of settlements in the occupied territories. we must go in a different direction, take up the spirit of oslo, take up the path of dialogue which created hope and led to the award of the nobel peace prize to the signatories of the agreements.
mr president, commissioner, in the gaza strip, 80% of the palestinians are dependent on humanitarian aid. more than half live in poverty and without health care. 80% of young people are unemployed. there are 47,000 palestinian political prisoners. israeli attacks continue in the occupied territories. civilians are still being killed. these numbers are shocking, of course they are. but behind these numbers are people. the strengthening of the influence of the extreme right in power in israel has sharpened the conflict, which increases the demand from the european union. that is why this resolution is important. because he insists that the two-state solution is the only possible solution for peace. because it calls for the convening of an international peace conference. because it advocates increased european funding to stimulate economic recovery, help rebuild infrastructure and support civil society. because it demands an end to illegal settlements and demands that palestinian ngos cease to be considered terrorists. a solution to the conflict must be found and the european union must be part of it.

madam president, commissioner, the eu-asean summit must be a moment to deepen regional cooperation and to expand trade relations with asean nations by diversifying supply chains, which is good for both blocs. asean is also a strategic partner for strengthening the eu's strategic autonomy in a multilateral framework. this summit is the moment to move from words to deeds, but it must also be an opportunity not to be missed to promote common and universal values with the governments of the region. we encourage the creation of a legal and political framework for respect for social, labour and environmental rights and for fostering support for civil society organisations in the region, countering the shrinking of civic space in several asean countries. we encourage the end of laws that limit rights such as freedom of association and assembly. above all, this summit must result in the strengthening of relations between the european union and the asean region and its member countries.

on behalf of the ppe group. madam president, the rising cost of living is an undeniable reality. the historical numbers of inflation are obvious, but it's critical to look at the people that those numbers represent. families, especially the youngest, are facing escalating food prices, significant instability in energy prices and a tremendous rise in housing credit. we know that invariably the poorest families are the most affected and that solutions cannot be delayed. the fight against inflation in europe is a political priority, an economic emergency and a social emergency. everybody has to do their part. in the european union, the independent european central bank leads monetary policy and the commission has a duty to ensure that its funds, the funds we approve here every year, reach the people and that there is free and fair competition within the internal market. wasting time on easy criticism of europe is not serious. it is a matter for those who have no solutions to present in their own country. unfortunately, this is the reality we see in some european capitals, particularly in my country, portugal. this fight is not fought by taxing more of the taxpayers who are already strangled. it is done with the good use of european funds to generate employment and investment, with responsible and ambitious national budgets and with more seriousness in political discourse. the time we live in is not for justification, it is for decision.
madam president, after a response to the pandemic crisis that seemed to indicate that europe had learned the lesson of the financial crisis, we are again heading for a self-imposed recession and economic crisis. the european central bank, contrary to its own analyses, responds to inflation by raising interest rates. the commission regrets the consequences of speculation in finance, distribution and energy, but it does nothing to stop it, and governments like mine freeze wages and pensions, imposing a definitive cut to all people who have lived or live by their work, while offering tax breaks to capital income. the economic recession in 2023 is not inevitable. we know what solutions have worked, and we also know what solutions have failed in the past. the budgetary and monetary policy of the european union must be reversed, otherwise we will have to answer to our citizens for having deliberately and knowingly provoked a new economic crisis in europe.
madam president, in portugal food products increased by 18.9%, bottled gas by 19.5% and energy products by 27.6%. there is a sharp increase in household incomes, 9.2% in lisbon and 6% in porto. the house benefit will go up between 100 and 150 euros and five families are evicted daily. it is estimated that more than two million people will put on the poverty numbers. this is the reality of portuguese workers. meanwhile, big business in distribution, energy and banking is making record profits at the expense of speculation in the prices of goods and services, low wages, precariousness, increased exploitation of labour and the impoverishment of the people. the general increase in wages and pensions is therefore an emergency. they can't be the same people who pay. it is an emergency because it is the only way people can cope with the steady and burdensome increase in the cost of living. it's an emergency because it stimulates the economy, stops unemployment and prevents a bigger crisis, and it's an emergency because it's a way to redistribute the wealth produced more fairly. there is no excuse for postponing the general increase in wages and pensions.
mr president, we all remember scandals in political campaigns such as the brexit or the trump campaign. they were linked to foreign interference, but also to new ways, especially digital, of campaigning. with the regulation of political advertising that we are working on here in parliament we can, at once, help to correct both of these problems. we want to ban the financing of political advertising by foreign actors in the european union. we want to increase transparency in campaigns by requiring information on who pays and how much for political advertising and by creating political ad repositories that allow journalists, researchers and public authorities to more easily detect foreign interference. and finally, we want to restrict the targeting of political advertising that, in the famous campaigns i mentioned, was used to polarize and radicalize many voters, manipulating their emotions with distorted or even completely false information. at a time when democracy is under attack, we must be brave in the remedy. believe me, that's what we're doing.
rapporteur. madam president, mr president, mr commissioner, two years after its entry into force, the multiannual financial framework 2021-2027 in the centralised management pillar has been brought to the limit of its budgetary availability. and this, alongside nextgenerationeu, has also enabled us to respond to the economic and social effects of the pandemic. unexpected challenges have come to the eu budget: support for ukraine in its different dimensions, support for people and businesses to mitigate the economic and social consequences of the war, the impact of energy and food prices, or the effect of rising inflation. it has worked, the principle of flexibility has worked, but we have already realised that the use of flexibility is insufficient. i am pleased to note the strength and political will of the european parliament as a budgetary authority, taking decisions in record time so that europeans have the necessary support from the european union. from vaccines to supporting ukrainian refugees, or supporting the financing of €18 billion for the functioning of the ukrainian state, so that it can provide minimum services to citizens. it is now clear that the architecture of the multiannual financial framework is not adapted to the pressure it is under, nor to the current macroeconomic timetable. the current mff, as it stands, is not equipped to finance emerging policies such as climate transition and compliance with the paris agreement, increased defence cooperation, the creation of a sovereign fund for strengthening the strategic autonomy of the european union, the building of energy autonomy, where repowereu has to become a key instrument, the implementation of the integrated circuits regulation, the social climate fund, which must be in the budget of the european union. we therefore need a revised multiannual financial framework. so we need to move from point solutions to structural solutions in the budget architecture. on the revenue side, we need the new own resources agreed in 2020 to be entered in the union budget on the agreed timetable. in order to enable the union budget to react quickly to crises and their social and economic effects, we propose the creation of a permanent instrument which can be activated whenever necessary. we therefore need the commission to propose a permanent instrument within the mff and we hope, commissioner, that this will be its legacy for the future of the union budget. commissioner hahn, the european parliament does not want to continue to see the creation of financial instruments outside the budget of the european union, in which parliament has seen its power of budgetary authority reduced. this led to the creation of the european stability mechanism in 2012 and, outside the community framework, and more recently, nextgenerationeu. enough with the 122nd! we also do not want funding outside the union budget, via external resources, we want more transparency, more democratic accountability. we also do not want cohesion policy to be used as a crisis response mechanism, as it has had to be now, and well, because there was no other way to compensate for the shortcomings in budgetary flexibility. i'm not going to. cohesion policy is not a crisis instrument, it is the investment policy of the european union and must remain so. commissioner hahn, as always, will have the full support of the european parliament to make the budget of the european union more efficient and more effective in supporting the political ambition of the european union.
rapporteur. madam president, at the end of this debate, it is clear that we need a revised mff, we need to protect cohesion policy and we need a permanent instrument within the budget of the european union. and we ask the commission, when preparing the revised proposal for the 2021-2027 mff, to take account of the political lines set out in this resolution, which are parliament's requests. i would like to conclude this debate by thanking mr olbrycht, who has prepared this report with me. jan, it's a pleasure working with you! i thank the shadow rapporteurs who helped to make this report ambitious and forward-looking, valérie hayer, rasmus andresen, dimitrios papadimoulis, the sectoral rapporteurs from the various committees, because they too had a say, the secretariat of the committee on budgets and our staff for the long hours and the quality of the work. thank you! we are making the european union's multiannual financial framework work in the service of the european union and its citizens.
author. madam president, after we watched the enthronement ceremony of xi jinping as the supreme leader, the seemingly impossible has happened. yes, there will always be hope as long as there are men and women who are able to rebel against any form of tyranny and cry out for freedom and rights. the protests of the chinese people regarding the covid-19 measures, calling for freedom and even the removal of xi jinping from power, demonstrate the will of the people to fight and forced the chinese government to backtrack on the lockdown measures. yes, it is possible. so let us draw conclusions and make human rights dialogues something effective and not just an expedient. let us be consistent in defending human rights in china. we do not turn a blind eye to the atrocities committed in xinjiang and tibet. let us not be fearless and calm in the face of the process of adopting in macau a national security law similar to that applied in hong kong, laws which violate the freedoms and guarantees enshrined in the sino-portuguese and sino-british declarations. let's not turn a blind eye to the way the tech platforms have been cooperating for the repressive control of the chinese people. and there will be no more extraditions to china between member states.
